Enclosure No. 1 to Despatch No. 631
dated September 13, 1949 from the
United States Political Adviser for
Japan, Tokyo, on the subject "Copies
UNCLASSIFIED
of Four Speeches on Subject of "How
Can Japan Become a Self-Supporting
Nation?"
(COFY)
"HOW CAN JAPAN BECOLE A SELF-SUPPORTING-NATION?"
Speech of DR. SHERWOOD M.. FIE, Director, Economics and
Planning, Economic and Scientific Section, on "America's Town
Meeting of the Air" from the Ernie Pyle Theater in Tokyo, Japan,
on August 30. 1949, to be broadcast over WJZ and the Americen
Broadcasting Company Network on September 27, 1949, ε:30 - 9:30
EST。
Mr Denny, we have all known for some time the tremendous
cost of modern warfare. However, it has only been since l945
that the United States has learned how costly the occupation of
a defeated, aggressor nation can be The United States in its
endeavor to democratize and aic Japan has been appropriating almost
500 million dollars annually for the last tvo years. The object
has been to rehabilitate that devastated nation and prevent disease
and social unrest among her population How long will the United
States sustain this burden? And, what can be done to speed up the
realization of a healthy, self-su:porting, democratic Japan?
You may be somewhat nystified as to how a nation which so
short a time ago was the dominant political, economic and military
power in the Far East should suddenly have been transformed into
its present dependence upon American assistance. How could Japan
have been so swiftly altered from its prewar position to its present
distressea economic condition? Here ere some of the facts I hope
you will keep in mind throughout tonight's discussion. First is
the destruction wrought by the war and the loss of her extensive
Asiatic empire. In additicn Japan has lost her merchant marine and
her assured sources of imports of cheap food and industrial raw
materials as well as a virtually assured market for her export
manufactures. Unhappily coinciding with the foregoing has been
nylon's displacement, in large pert, of silk. This was Japan's
chief prewar export and prime source of dollars with which to buy
American goods. Eighty per cent of Japan's cotton textile industry
was scrapped for metal and consumed by the war machine. Foreign
competition for the Asiatic market, moreover, is far keener than
was the case in the prewar period.
Fundamental
UNCLASSIFIED

=== Page 9 === (confidence 0.98)
Enclosure No. 1
to Tokyo's Despatch No. 631,
September 13, 1949.
- 2 -
Fundamental to Japan's economic problem is her small size and
exceedingly poor natural resources. Japan's eighty-one million
population, swollen from her 194l population of 72 million, 1s
crowded into a territory smaller than the State of California. With
only 16 per cent of her area fit for cultivation, she is burdened
with the most sericus population problen of any nation in the world.
Imagine what it means to this smallarea to sustain an increase of
1, 300,000 persons annually. Each successive year will witness a
growing gulf between her harvests and the needs of her growing
numbers
Before the war Japan was known as the "workshop of the Orient".
She was the unchallenged industrlal leader in the Far East and
managea by her export of manufactured products end earnings of her
merchant fleet and other services to earn the income to pay for
her necessary imports. These were purchesed predominantly from her
neighbors and consisted of food, cotton, fertilizer, petroleum, iron
ore, metals, wood pulp and other industrial raw materials. Japan
continues to be the mcst econonical source of cheap manufactured
goods for her Oriental neighbors though she is increasingly chal-
lenged by the competition of England and other countries.
Currently, Japan's exports are running at an annual rate of
some 500 nillion dollars while her imports, thanks to American aid,
are running at a rate of about go0 million dollars. The problem
consists of bridging this gap of 400 million dollars.
The Communist control of China, Menchuria and North Korea has
profoundly affected Japan's trade prospects. Total economic activi-
ty iu Asia, moreover, has not yet approached prewar levels. Japan
has a vital stake in overall Oriental economic activity. Before
the war, Japan secured over 55 per cent of all her inports from
Asie. Currently, she is getting well under 20 per cent from her
Asiatic neighbors. This unbulanced condition must be corrected.
A prosperous, self-supporting and democretic Japan will be most
difficult of realization in a poverty stricken, Conmunist dominated
Orient
What actions then are necessary to enable Japan to become
self-supporting? First, Japan must redouble her efforts to in-
crease her exports through more intensive sales promotion, improve-
ment of products, and streamlining of production technics.
Second, Jupan must increase the output of her farms, her mines,
the fishing cutch of her neighboring seas and such other natural
resources as she possesses, through adoption of more modern and
efficient practices.
Third,
UNCLASSIFIED

=== Page 10 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure No. 1
to Tokyo's Despatch No. 631,
September 13, 1949.
- 3 -
Third, existing restrictive trade barriers and discriminatory
treatment of Japah's products by foreign netions should be termina-
ted
Fourth, Japan must be enabled to carry more of her imports
and exports on her own vessels thereby saving very substantial
shipping costs.
Fifth, Jupan must develop her potentially sizable tourist
trade and encourage foreign investment to bring in foreign exchange.
Sixth, Japan must shift her procurement of food and raw materials
to tnose areas which require her manufactured export products and
can supply her import requirements.
Seventh, Japan and her Asiatic neighbors, with the aid of expert
technological assistance, must work together to utilize the vast
undeveloped rescurces and industrial capacity of the Asiatic main-
land. Thrcugh increased output of food and raw materials, Japan's
neighbors will acguire the means to pay for such needed manufactured
goods as Japan is best situated to furnish.
Every American has a vital interest in a seif-supporting Japan
which is essential to a peaceful, den.ocratic Asia, free of Com-
munist domination

"HOW CAN JAPAI BHCOLE A SELF-SUPTORTHNG NATION?"
Speach of MR. AKIA KODAKI. Administrator-General of Inter-
national Trude, on "Anerica's Town Leeting of the Air" from the
Ernie Pyl• Theater in Tokyo Jupan on August 30. 1949, to be
broadcast over WJZ and the American Eroudcastiug Company network
on Septemter 7 1949. 8:30 . 9:30 EST
May I first express to the listeners in the United Stutes the
deep appreciation of the Japanese people for all that the United
States has done for us during the past four years?
Anyone with the least sense of self-respect strongly desires
to lead a self-supporting existence, rether thar being uependent
upon his friends Such is the fecling now enterteined by the ma-
jority of the people in this counrty
However, Dr Fine haa just told you a urber of difriculties
we have to face in the way of our self-suy orting eccncy
Ir.
spite of all. can we ever become selr-sup orting?
I should say "Yes " Then. now? I buse my conviction upon
the fect that we have vust resoorees of man-power, man-power with
tio desire to work Besides, I van toll you that there is no other
country in Asia which has so may skilled laborers in proportion
to the population.
Therefore our self-suyporting economy nust be based on the
utaost utilization of cur man-power. Only through the sales of
industrial products can Japan secure the means of payment for im-
porting focdstuffs and raw materials so urgently needea in Japan.
We have lready nace considerable progress in this line. We
have signed trude agreements of varicus fcrns with nearly 20 coun-
tries, including Greut britain, France, Sweden, Australia, Inuia
and other British Jominions, Dutch East Indies, Korea ana several
South Americun countries. How, take, for example, figures of our
exports.
In 1946, our exports amountea only to a little over 100
million
This increused to $l70 million in 1947 and 260 million
in 1948
Furthermore, the figure for the first half of this year
has risen

to Tokyo's Despatch No. 631,

has risen well over that for the whole of last year. These figures,
I believe, will indicate the fact that both the Government and the
people of Japan are firmly deternined to realize a self-supporting
economy, and are actually doing all that they can towards that end.
There are nany things yet to be acomplished: first, wider
participation of our merchant marine in internaticual trade; then,
the conclusion of reciprocal traue greements with adaitional
countries; the gredual diversion of our imports from the estern
Worlu to the Far East; the recover: of our best markets in prewar
days wnich ure now almost lost to us, such as Communist dominated
Manchuria, North Korea and China; more foreign investment and more
intensified tourist industry in Japan, the participation in the
International Trade Organization ana in the International Monetary
Fund, etc All these things are of critical importance to us, but
none of these can be achieved by our unaided efforts.
Therefore, we do hope that the world vd il sympathize with our
legitimate aspiration for a self-supporting economy. Let us nope
that the world will permit us to embark upon shess prograis and
allow us to have a chance for fair ccmpetition in foreign coun-
tries - each country admitting cur products on a non-diserininating
basis and our businessmen on ecual terns ith otners. On our part,
we will do our best to res, ond to their good will in every possible
way and to refrain fren cennitting any aet which may be considered
as unfair competition or aumping.
Only in this fashion cun Jupan become a self-suyporting und
consequently truly democretic and peuce-loving nation.

"HOW JA. JAA
"IO.?"
Speech of AR. KIICnTRO SATOi. President or the Leikoku Bank,
Lte., on "America's Town Keeting cf the Air" fror the Ernis Pyle
Theater in Tokyo, Japan, on August 30, 1949, to be broadcast cver

Dr, Fine has explained very vividly the econonic plight which
Japan is now facing, especially the gloomy picture of our population
crowded in a devastated area I think we must first of all do the
utmost within our power to settle tnis alarming increase of popula-
tion adopting, among other things, birth-control particularly in
rural districts. With our populatiou increase, it is almost im-
possible to make out a plan to overtake the consequent increase of
national requiraments even for a bare subsistence level. If the in-
creasing tendency of our population could be arrested somehow, I
should think that a half of the problem was solved.
As Mr. Kodaki pointed out, I am fully in accord with him in
regard to our merchant marine to be robuilt and the diversified
trade agreements with additional countries of trading possibility.
I would add that Japan needs to enlarge her fishing area to increase
the supply of food-stuffs. particularly that of protein which is
very short.
It is unfcrtunately true that the efficiesej of our labor has
declined somewhat as compared with the prewur stuidard. This was
inevitable due to the depressed stu dard of livi g which followed
the War Although ür Koaaki spoke of our maln-power es our assets,
it was only recently that peoyle recovered from vacuum-minded
state and came to think of becoming self-supporting Noreover,
labor is liable to become susceptible to cormunistic propaganda.
While this movement is now being combatted by the predominantly
democratic lemdership in the labor movement, there is no more
effective solution than giving them a higher standard of living.
This can be accomplished by increasing foreign trade and conseguemt-
Ly employment.
However, as Dr. Fine has pointed out, our foreign trede sheme
highly unfavorable balance with the Taited states. Japen will be
obliged to

sale of prouuets abroad in both the dollar und nen-dcllar areas as
the only means or prccurenent cf surficient exchange with which to
purchase rccas and essentiel ruw nateriels This is a land whose
greatest asset is skil e. und seni-skilled manpower, a ranpower
which must be utilizea to the utnost in peuceful pursuits if it
is to avoid resort again to aggressive intants, The Japanese are
no more iamune fror resert te extremes, ineluding the false appeals
of Soviet Communien, then any cther discontented underprivileged

=== Page 16 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure No. 4
to Tokyo's Despatch No. 631,
September 13, 1949.
-2
Does not logic demand, therefore, an appeal to every legiti-
mate element of recovery, no matter how small. Americans, adjusted
to bigness are frequently inclined to igrore the little things,
which can be so important in the lives of the Jupanese.
With regara to agriculture, I cannot accept the implication
involved in the statement that only sixteen percent of Japanese
soil is available for cultivation Thero are still relatively
large areas of uplands lying fallow because the devastating bamboo
grass has not been eradicated. It may be true that rice culture
is reaching its limit, but there is clear evidence that production
on a larger scale of those grains of higher nutrition value could
reduce still further the margin between food supplies and mouths
to feed. Is it not true, moreover, that mountain sides and
plateaus of these exquisite isles ere still not being properly ex-
ploited? The Swiss could teach the Japanese much about mountain
fed cattle, reforestation and proper utilization of waterpower
resources. No economy can properly function without adequate trans-
port, and the lack of roads in rural areas continues to hamper the
distribution of products
Another item of real importunce relates to the brain power of
this land The purge of Japanese of nigh war position by all in-
clusive categories, resulted in the inclusion of many victims of
undoubted integrity who are now deprived of giving their great
talents to the soluticn of their nution's peace time problems.
Military necessity may have demanded drastic measures, but the
time has come in my judzment, to hasten the process of unpurging
those who have been undeserving of such penalties.
But in its final analysis it is evident that no permenent
solution of the Japanese problem of self support cen be had without
relating it closely to the overall problem of the Far East. Just
as Germany belongs to Europe so does Japan belong to Asie. But
unless and until Americe cen develop a Far East policy which com-
prehends not only basic issues of the aree, but the inter-relation-
ship of things, the Japanese probles. itself cannot be solved. In
this respect it may very well be that the Japanese themselves, who
know this area of the world much better than we. can be of help if
their help is earnestly sought.

Sub ct: Forwarding Findings or Survey Regarding Public
FE.un.
Interest in Purchase or Zaibatsu Stocks.
PCSn
0.1
COR
The Acting Political Adviser has the honor to forward five
copies of a public opinion survey entitled "The Purchase of
Zaibatsu Stocks: an Appraisal of Public Interest," and released
on August 31, 1949, by the Public Opinion and Sociological Re-
search Division, Civil Information and Education Section, General
Headquarters, Supreme Commander for the Allied Powers.
The enclosed survey, which was prepared to assist in the dis-
posal of "Zaibatsu" securities and which is the result of inter-
views with over 5,000 persons, comes to the gloomy conclusion that
nine out of ten persons either know nothing of or have mistaken
ideas about the "securities democratization program," and that
"interest in the program is slight."
The survey notes, however, that there is "some good will" to-
ward the program, and concludes that a wider base of stockholders
has been built up than existed prior to the war.

4.Produstion program
Mining and manufacturing
In order to bring about "a self-sustaining bgonomy
makes possible a rational econamic circulation" rationalis
tion and further expansion of industrial production are nades
sary first of all. Furthermore that such development.muat
espocially in the heavy and chemical industries is,a marle
cept which appears throughout this:plan.
When setting up a production plan under such a poliot the
first problem that arisee 1s the fact that such a plan must n
exceed the limits.of capital accumulation. In drawing up thia,
plan, we have tried to confine it within the limibs of ompital
accumulating capacity depending on the policies followed. we
feel that it would be more difficult in the early period of the
plan rather than in the latter.
The second point which must be taken into consideration is
to utilize existing facilities as much as poastble with an em-
phasis upon repair in order to make the most effective use of
limited materiels or to lay emphasis on those mhien yield high
est returms on investment. This does not mean, however, the
more restoration of tho industrial structure of fapilitiea to
the pre-war. Considering their relation to their competitive
power in international markets, steps must be devised to puah
ahead the modermization of the facilities and to advanee the
industrial stmucture.

6. Employment
Full employment not only is the biggest problem in the
Western countries efter the war, but 'also must be the high-
st ideel of our economic policy. However, in our country
suffering from the relative overpopulation, the unemploy.
ment problem has the close connection with the low level or
living.
Unemployment in this country hes been veiled by poor
social security system, traditional family system, existence
of enormous small business end blackmarket economy acoompany-
ing inflation, and it has remeined as latent overpopulation
adversely affecting living standard, insteed of energing as
anconcualod unemployment. Consequantly = large number of con
cealod  unemployed are oxisting urier the strango phenomenon
of overpopulation in enterprise, turm eud eoher organizatione,
In which each shares very low stanter t living. Those
who ought to be unemploye muct be uncevered and become un-
employed. It is essentiel to increase labour productivity
through this,process.

In conseguenee of tho, imblementation cf the economio
stebrlization policies based on the Nine-Point Economio
Stabilization Program and the establishment of a singl
exchange rate, unemployment is elready tending to be un-
veiled and increased. Such reorganization process
accompanying inevitably ta nermelization of the Japanese
economy, if pushed toc fer or if not attended by effective
unemployment measures, will ferment social unrest and
defeat the very object cf ncrmalization of economy. It
will, therefore, be necessary to make necessary adjustment
from time to time as the aconomic situation may require.
Thus, employment, supported by high labour produotivity,
must become e piller of expcrt expansion and production
increase.  At tho same time, it must be planned always to
inorease employment further and further. Moreover,
employnent increese must be a real one based on rational
distribution of workers among industrtes which take full
account of ability and personality of each person. In
-other words, the central probler: of the employment pro-

of employment but also "ur pro luctivity.

=== Page 171 === (confidence 0.98)
Introduction
The securities democratization program is an important part of the ov
all Occupation policy of shifting the ownership of Japan's industry from a
amall number of large owmers (the "Zaibatau") to a wider base among the
people as a whole. Under the program, Zaibatsu and other stocs were made
available for public purchase. In crder to assist the Securities Coordi-
nating Liquidation Conmission, a Japanese Government agency, to evaluate the
program's present state of progress, and to test the results of the stock
purchase information program, the Jiji News Agency Public Opinion Room con-
ducted a nationwide survey during the last two days of Aprii l949.1 Over
5,000 persons were individually interviewed concerning their experiences
with, and their opinions concerning, the purchase of these stocks. The re-
spondents were selected by methods vhich, it is believed, yield a scientifi-
cally accurate cross section of the adult population of Japan.
1. During the same period in 194, the same agency conducted a similar
survey in the Kansai area. The results of this survey are reported upon
in the publication "Attitudes in the Kansai District Toward Democratization
of Securities," Current Japanese Public Opinion Surveys, No. 10, 26 August
1948, CIE, GHQ, SCAP.
i

=== Page 172 === (confidence 0.99)
SOMEMAJOR FINDINGS OF THE SURVEY
THERE ARE SOME GAPS IN PUBLIC IIFORATION
Almost nine out of every 10 persons either know nothing
of the securities democratization program or have mis-
taken ideas about its nature.
Approximately nine out of 10 persons are unable to identify
the principal institutions engaged in the stock democratiza-
tion program; between 96 and 99 out of every 100 persons
are completely unable to indicate the functions of these
institutions.
Three out of every five persons are unable to identify a
single one of the rights of stockholders.
Less than one out of every eight persons is aware of the
employees' priority in the purchase of liquidated Zaibatsu
stocks.
INTEREST IN THE PROGRAL IS SLIGHT
More than seven out of every 10 persons expressed abso-
lutely no interest in stocks and bonds.
Three out of every four persons never read stock reports
in the newspapers.
Almost nine out of every l0 persons never listen to radio
stock news.
For eight out of every persons, the purchase of stocks
is not included in their plans for the future.
HOWEVER, THERE IS SOIE GOOD .ILL TOMARD THE PROGRAM
Although most people were unable to offer a judgment, a
majority of those who do have opinions feel that the
securities democratization program will have beneficial
effects upon
industrial rehabilitation,
economic stabilization, and
the attraction of foreign capital into Japan.
ii

=== Page 173 === (confidence 0.98)
INFORMATIOI: IS DMPORTANT
It has an effect upon present stockovming:
Among all people, slightly less than one out of every eight
owms stock.
BUT.
Among people who know a great deal about the securities
democratization program, almost one out of every two owns
stocks.
Among people who know a great deal about stocks in
general, two out of every five own stocks.
It has an effect upon people's stock purchase intentions:
Among all people, only threc out of every lo0 can be con-
sidcred to have a "high" intention to buy stocks,
BUT..
Among people who know a great deal about the securities
democratization progran, nine times as many can be rated
as "high" in purchase intention -- 27 out of every 100.
Among people who know a  reat deal about stocks in general,
the same number - 27 out of every 100 -- can be rated as
"high" in stock purchase intontion.
PERSONAL EXPERIECE ITH STOCKS IS VERY IPORTANT
It has an effect upon future intentions:
Anon all people, only three out of every l00 can be con-
sidered to have a "high" intention to buy stocks,
mong persons actually owning stocks at the present tine,
12 out of every l00 indicated a "high" intention to pur-
chase more stocks.

=== Page 174 === (confidence 0.99)
INCOLE LEVEL HAS AN IAPORTANT RELATION TO RECEPTIVITY TO THE PROGRAM
In general, respondents of lowor income groups, as con-
trasted with those of higher ones:
Owm stocks less frequently
Are less interested in stocks
Know less about stocks
Know less about the securities democratization program
Express less intention to purchase stocks in the near future
HOEVER..
There aro some indications that the rate of stock ownership
among lower income groups has increased substantially above
that which prevailed before the war.

Chapter I
PERSOHAL EXPERIECE ITH STOCHS D BONDS
A. The Stockowmers
One of the principal factors which affects the thinking and behavior
of people in relation to stocks and bonds is their personal experience.
The more experience pcople have with stocks and bonds, the more they tund
to knov about them, the less they feel that stock owning is a completely
strangc world, and the more they pay attention to publicity and information.
Also, if people have had relatively satisfactory cxperiences with stocks
and bonds in the past, they are likely to be more favorable toward informa-
tion und publicity designed to induce them to purchase now. Thorofore,
this survey oxemined the matter of personal experience in considerablo detail.
More than one person in 10 owns
stocks, but the onership rat
is very uneven in different
sectors of tho population
Thile about 12 percent of tihe adult population reported somo stock
owmership, there are so.me notable variations in the rate of ownership:
While 45 percent of the business managerial

own stocks;

own stocks, only cignt percent of men under
30 own stocks;
hile 32 percent of ursons with college ed-
ucation own stocks, only cight percent of
persons with no more than elementary school
education report stock omership.
Whon past stock ownersiip is examined, the same goneral tondencies may
be seen. It is also undoubtudly the case that the respondunts of uppor
income status have a larger total amount of stock ownership than respondents
of lower income status. However, the survey figures refer only to the total
number of stockoumers, regardless of the amount of stocks orned.

From this point of view, the fact that 29 percent of the respondents reported
that they had friends and relatives who were stockowners is important in es-

whose environment contains stockowners tend to be more receptive to stock
purchase programs than do people without such a background.

B. The Honstockowners
The great bulk of the population are, of course, not stockowners.
Eighty-two percent of the respondents had never owned stocks, and at the
present time 88 percent are nonstockoners. In order to spread the securi-
ties democratization program more videly among the people, it is essential
to understand the motives of tihe nonstockowners.

and unfamiliar with stocks, or they
feel that they are not able to afford them
For many people, especially of lower cconomic status, the world of
stocks and bonds is remote from their daily life. Even if they had the
money available, stockownership appears to them to be completely unrelated
to their normal patterns of economic behavior.

never given it any thought." (Lower
secondary school teacher in Nagasaki)

ing stocks." (Railway track laborer,
Tolyo)

And some people feel that stocks

Chapter II
KNOWLEDGE OF STOCKS AND THE SECURITIES D.: OCRATIZATION PROGRAM

Appendix I
THE SURVEY LETHOD
In the present survey, approxi ately 5,42ó adults were interviewed
throughout Japan. A ratio of one in 5,000 was used for sampling in urban
areas, and a ratio of one in 1C, 000 was used in rural areas. A standard
statistical weighting procedure was applied in such a way that rural re-
turns were counted twice in the tabulation of the final results. Respondents
were interviewed in 3l6 separate localities throughout the country. These
included 106 villages, 86 towns, and 124 cities, including the six largest
cities of Japan. The sample areas were selected by random probability methods
which tend to eliminate bias. Within the sample areas, respondents were se-
lected by ..ec.anical rundom methods from household registers.
A majority of the respondents was interviewed at their homes, a few at
their place of ork. ihile some questions required simple "yes" and "no"
answers, others elicited more elaborate statements from the respondents,
which were recorded as fully as possible by the interviewers. The completed
questionnaires were returned to the central office of Jiji Hews  ency Public
O inion Room for analysis and tabulation.
In the analysis, an atteapt was made to set up several ratings ou issues
which could not easily be analyzed on the basis of the responses to single
questions. These include the following:
1. Rating of interest in stocks.
High:
Respondents who regularly read the stock market
cuotation section of the neuspaper or who listen
to stock cuotations on the radio.
Medium:
Respondents vho read or listen occasionally to
stock news.
Lew:
Respondents who, as a rule, neither read nor
listen to stock news.
2. Rating of stock purchase intentions:
ligh:
Respondents who indicated a desire to purchase
stocks, and either expressed an intention of
buying in the near future or had a definite
idea of the stocks they intended to buy.
21

=== Page 196 === (confidence 0.97)
Medium:
Respondents who indicated a desire to purchase
stocks, but who were undecided about the time
of purchase, or had no intention of buying in
the near future because thcy could not afford
to or knew nothing about stocks.
Low:
Respendents who had no desire to buy stocks.
3. Rating of knowledge of the sucurities denocratization program:
Points were assigned to correct answers on the basis of a
rough comion sense weighting as follows:

DATE: September 27, 1949
FROM
FE
- Mr. Butterworth
B
SUBJECT:
Operation of Japanese Recovery Program by the
Economic Cooperation Administration.
I. The Problem
ReaurruG
1. The FY 195l Japanese appropriation request will be submitted
to the Congress in January. If language reflecting an ECA take-over
is not associated with that request, before it is passed on by the
(3)
3.4.50/
Bureau of the Budget, the prospects of an ECA take-over until after
June, 195l, will be greatly lessened. The Department of State, the
Department of National Defense, and the ECA should, therefore, reach
agreement as soon as possible concerning the desirability and practic-
ability of an ECA take-over.
II. Recommendation
(i)
2. It is recommended that you send the attached letters to
2749
Secretary Gray and Mr. Hoffman.
III. Discussion
3. ECA Take-Over in Washington. Effective transfer to ECA of
responsibility for the expenditure of funds approprated for Japanese
import procurement would require that the following functions now
performed by the Army Department in Washington be performed instead
by the ECA in Washington:
(a) final determination of the amounts and types of goods
 C
to be bought for Japan out of funds appropriated for this purpose by
the Congress, and final determination of the sources from which and
the prices at which these goods should be procured;
(b) participation in the formulation of U.S. Governmental
policies relating to Japanese recovery;
(c) participation in U.S. Governmental determination of the
894.50/9-2749
amount of funds to be requested of the Congress for Japanese import
procurement during the fiscal years following FY 1951.
RM/R
It is
CONFIDENTIAL
51+.1.
Cat
1,

=== Page 204 === (confidence 0.99)
CONFIDENTIAL
-2-
It is believed that an ECA assumption of these Washington functions
might result in a more effective discharge of them than has hitherto been
obtained from the Army Department. It seems likely that the experience,
organization, and personnel of the ECA in Washington would be more suited
than those of the Army Department to supervising the expenditure of U.S.
funds for Japamese self-support.
It should also be pointed out that many of the decisions that must
be reached in Washington in eonnection with the Japanese recovery
program will affect, and will be affected by, decisions made here con-
cerning U.S. foreign aid programs in Europe and the Far East. A greater
coordination between these various decisions would therefore seem
desirable. In view of ECA's responsibility for U.S. foreign aid programs,
such greater coordination might be associated with an effective tranafer
of Washington functions relative to the expenditure of funds appropriated
for Japanese import procurement to the BCA.
4. ECA Representation in Japan. In Germany, under Military Govern-
ment, the ECA mission was without the responsibilities that characterized
CA missions in other countries, these responsibilities being largely
assumed by General Clay. The ECA's ability to perform its authorized
functions with respect to German rehabilitation was adversely affected
by this fact. An effective BCA take-over in the case of Japan would
require a more satisfactory arrangement with respect to ECA representa-
tion in Japan. Indeed agreement on this matter, defined in rather
concrete terms, is a necessary prerequisite to acceptance of the take-
over idea by SCAP or by the ECA. Such an arrangement might be one under
which:
(a) General MacArthur would be appointed by the U.S. Government
as ECA representative in Japan, assuming this duty concurrently with his
duties as Supreme Commander for the Allied Powers and as Commander-in-
Chief, U.S. Forces in the Far East;
(b) Mr. Hoffman would then designate an ECA official whom
General MacArthur would appoint as his Deputy for CA matters, and this
Deputy would recruit a staff to perform those duties of SCAP which relate
to economic recovery and stabilization.
It is believed that the resultant qualitative improvement in the
economic advice given to SCAP at the highest levels would have beneficial
results that might permit some reduction in the number of SCAP economic
personnel at the working level. For ECA would probably be able to staff
its mission with persons of great er competence and prestige than have so
far teen willing to work in Japan under Army Department auspices. Japan's
attainment of self-support might thus be facilitated as a consequence of
these persons' work in Japan.
5. Relation
CONFIDENTIAL

=== Page 205 === (confidence 0.99)
2
CON FI DEN TIAL
-3-
5. Relation of ECA Take-Over to the Present Regime of Control.
An ECA take-over, as defined in paragraphs 3 and 4, above, would involve
no change in the present regime of control for Japan and so would not
require intemational approval.
(a) The BCA's assumption of certain Washington functions
currently being discharged by the Army Department would represent merely
a reallocation within the U.S. Government of that Government's present
executive functions with respect to Japan. The nature of these functions
would remain unchanged, as would the policy prerogatives of the State
Department within the U.S. Govemment and the obligations of that Govern-
ment as a wrole to the Far Eastern Commission.
(b) The ECA Deputy Representative in Japan referred to in
paragraph 4 would not have any authority except that which he derived
from SCAP; his presence in Japan would thus not alter SCAP's authority.
in the eyes of the Japanese. Nor would this Deputy's presence relieve
SCAP of any of his obligations under established U.S. or FEC policies;
it would be incumbent on SCAP to ensure that the BCA take-over did not
impair the effectiveness of his staff in carrying out internationally
approved economic policies in regard to such matters as reform and
reparations.

A BIRD'S EYE VIEW OF JAPAN'S
MAJOR ECONOLIC PROBLELS
Population and Food
1. SCAP, understandably, never tires of pointing out that
Japan, with a population of 8l.9 million, has a total area smaller
than that of the State of Celifornia. Of that area no more than
15.7 per cent is under cultivation, whilst 4.2 per cent affords
pasture of a kind. Forests, officially, cover no less than 67.4
per cent of the erea. The density of the population in terms of
arable land (15 million ecres) is eleven times that of the United
States.

2. Through the intensive utilisation of her resources Japan has
maintained her food imports over the last twenty years, grosso modo,
at an average of 3 million tons a year, thus meeting 85 per cent of
her total food requirements by end large from local production.
Imports of rice between 1936 and 1942 averaged 1.9 million tons an-
nually to supplement an average locel production of 9.7 million tons.
In l945, however, imports of food overall fell to 1.5 million tons
and local rice production to 6.4 million, with the result that Japan
faced the worst food shortage in modern times. (Last year Japan's
total imports of rice amounted to 50, 0oo tons from Siam.) The

Inflationary Pressure
17. Although industrial production has been gradually increasing,
as shown above, this achievement has been at a cost quite out of line
with world prices, financed of course by the generous assistance pro-
vided by United States and the consequent supply to local consumers of
foodstuffs and raw materials at prices whioh have borne no relation to
their real value. Labour and management have failed notahly to regover
their pre-war efficlency and there has been much diversion to domestio
consumption of manufactures intended for erport.
18. The slow increase in industrial output has been in marked
contrast with the expansion of the currency resulting mainly from the
cost of the Occupation and of the schemes advanced by the latter in its
Utopian zeal; the repairs to the devaststio: of war and of such
natural scourges as earthquakes, floods and typhoons; Government lending
to cover the deficit operations of basic industries; fiscal irregu-
larities; and last but not least the overtaking of prices by wages at
the end of 1948. The note issue from Yen 55 billion at the end of 1945,
rose year by year to Yen 93 - Yen 219 - Yen 355 billion at the end of
1948. Between June and December of last year the Tokyo consumer price
index (prepared by the Oriental Econonist, April 1946 - March 1947 = 100)
rose from 383 to 459 (20%).
19. At the beginning of this year, however, a happier relationship
was established between supply and demand. The note issue fell to
Yen 295 billion at the end of July and was still at this figure at the
end of August. The consumer price index had risen to 506 by the end of
June, but started to decline thereafter, being 50l at the end of July.
General Headquarters officials claim that they have now got the better
of the inflation, with particular stress on the fact thet wages have
remained comparatively stable during the last six months. (The Occu-
pation has found the need to control wages, if price controls were not
to be invalidated, particularly embarrassing in view of the encouragement
it has naturally given the labour movament. There are said to be 6
million trade unionists in Japan today. Another difficult series of
decisions concerns the extent of the stringency to be imposed on domestic
consumption levels.)
The Economic Stabilisation Plan
20. The check to the inflationary trend is in no small measure
due to the introduction of this plan (announced in December last) and
to the schrewdness and determination of Mr. Dodge, the Detroit banker
assigned by President Truman to advise General MacArthur on its imple-
mentation. The plan, which appears to have been evolved in Washington,
was intended to introduce a keynote of austerity and realiam in local
economio administration and planning, and to pave the way for the intro-
duction of a single rate of exchange in lieu of a yen-dollar ratio for
imports averaging l30 with a comparatively small spread, and one for
exports averaging 400 with a spread from 200 to 600! By the end of
May, after a three month stay in Japan, Mr. Dodge had succeeded in:-
(a) Forcing

=== Page 218 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure No. 1
to Tokyo's Despatch No. 679,
September 29, 1949.
(a)
Forcing the Jepanese Government to pass a budget
which integrated national, local and special expenditures
in one balanced account. This budget, although it allows
200 million yen for import and domestic subsidies, put an
end to direct export subsidies.
(b)
Instituting a policy of financial retrenchment and
strict control of credit, intended to provoke the rational-
isation of industry.
(c)
Establishing an official exchenge rate of Yen 360 to
the dollar (equivalent to 1,450.80 to the pound sterling)
at which rate all foreign trade transactions are being
conducted. Imported capital can be sold at this rate but
renittances of foreign exchenge are virtually confined to
payments for imports.
21. Results have been remarkably rapid. The reduction in the note
issue, the flattening of the price spiral, the export trend have all been
briefly dealt with above. The dismal Japanese prognostications of the
effects on exports of the new rate of exchange have not been borne out
as yet; anyway, this too was intended to play its part in the forcible
rationalisation of industries which have been living casually (and
often dishorestly, no doubt) on subsidies of all kinds and the fanciful
rates of exchange applied to their exports. hile rationalisation is
proceeding in certain industries - the registered industrial employ-
ment at the end of ay was no more than 400, 000 - the Government has
started to discharge a portion of its enorrous army of civil servants
and transport employees.
22. One of the most important problens besetting Japanese in-
dustry today is the tight money situation, to be attributed mainly to
(a) the tax collection drive, although there is still much laxity in
this respect (an American mission has just issued its recommendations
for the revision of the taxation system); and (b) the complete stoppage
of loans by the Reconstruction Finance Bank, loans largely dissipated
in the deficit financing of current production instead of being used as
intended for capital investment. In place of the R.F.B. Ir. Dodge
instituted a Counterpart Fund which is to receive the yen equivalent of
all U.S. aid imports (about Yen 140 billion in the current U.S.
financial year), these yen funds to be employed in an industrial in-
vestment programme of Yen 52 billion and the amortisation of Government
bonds held by the cormercial banks to a value of 62 billion. An
acditional 25 billion are to be held in reserve to be used as the
Supreme Cormander sees fit. The Fund, therefore, will provide a sub-
stantial amount of credit the use of which can be pretty rigidly
controlled by SCAP and the Japanese Government in collusion to meet
the most pressing investment needs of the country.
23. There

23. There is a campaign on foot, backed by the Japanese Boonomie
Stabilisation Board, to reverse "the Dodge polioy" on the grounds thet
disinflation is already degenerating into deflation; but the argments
advanced in support of this theory appear to be those of the type of
pusiness man who was doing very well out of American laviohness and who
can have no sympathy for economic discipline. There is certainly no
justifiable cause for back-pedalling and that is the declered view of
the Japanese Government. It will, however, be necessary for Scar to
guard against conditions of slump being created when the full disin-
flationary consequences of this policy make themselves. felt.
Continuance of American Aid and Viebility
24. r. Voorhees, the Under Secretary of the U.S. my, stated
in Tokyo on the loth September, that "from the funds appropriated and
being this year appropristed by Congress, the Army has bought over a
four year period or will buy before the end of the present fiscal year
 grand total of over one billion nine hundred million of relief
supplies for Japan and the Ryukyus alone." He thought that approprie-
tions would be reduced by 20% in the fiscal year beginning on the lst
July nert, or perhaps a little more, and still accomplish their.
eseential purposes as Japan's sconomic recovery proceeds. "I think
that can'be done and it should be done." He refused to prediot the
date of final balance when Japan could be sconomically self-supporting
but believed it would come "beoause there is great vitality and willing-
ness to work on the part of the Japanese people." He saw no reason why
Americen aid and the supervision of that aid should be affeoted by a
peace treaty: "A peace trecty alone isn't going to fesd people."
25. Some SCAP officials and many Japanese doubtless oling to the
hope thet 1953 will witness a really viable Japanese sconomy. This was
argued as feasible in a Five Year Plan prepared twelve months ago by
the Japanese Eoonomic Stabilisation Board, with a standard of living
(allowing for an inoreese in the population of close on 20 million)
approximating that of 1930/4. But the Doard, which has bitterly opposed
the Economic Stabilisation Plan as enforoed by Mr. Dodge, is revising
its caloulations. It has stated that these have been completely upset
by the recent introduction of the new regime of austerity - to aay
nothing presumably of adyerge factors in world trading conditions. It
1s certainly diffioult to envisage Japan living off its own resources
as early as l953 without considerable assistance from the United States,
if only in the form of loans on exceptionally easy terms of repeyment
which the United States, with their political and strategic interests
in this part of the world, would be only too willing to give her. And
even then Japan will probably go through a series of reourring crises-
of the type which the Sterling Area is now experienoing.

=== Page 254 === (confidence 0.99)
7
TO
: NA - Mr. Allison
DATE: October 21, 1949
Mr. Fearey RAU
SUBJECT: Turnover of Economic Responsibilities for Japan to ECA.
E's proposal is that we discuss with the Army and ECA the desirability of a
take-over by ECA before July l950 of the Army's responsibility for the Japanese
redovery program, including GARIOA, and that agreement to this be sought before
the FY 195l appropriation request is submitted to the Congress in January.
When a treaty of peace does come into effect, which could conceivably be
soon after July l950, the ECA will doubtless be called upon to administer a
894.50
continuing U.S. aid program. It is therefore desirable in any event that the
appropriation act contain language allowing a transfer of funds to ECA in the
eventa treaty during FY 1951.
Since ECA will eventually takeover aid responsibilities in Japan, there
would seem to be decided advantages to its doing so as soon as possible: a) in
easing the transition when the regime of control changes b) in placing aid
program responsibility in a single Washington agency c) in giving SCAP an able
economic adviser, such as Mr. Dodge, who has the confidence of both SCAP and this
Government d) in helping to get GH, SCAP out of detailed control of the Japanese
economy.
The guestion is whether SCAP would look with favor upon a drastic change
in his headguerters and responsibilities before the treaty, with the Army unlikely
to favor the change unless SCAP epproves. The advantages are not so great that
the Department of State would wish to engage in a time-consuming controversy
(not that Mr. Thorp has necesserily suggested that), if it can prevail only with
difficulty or not at all.
If the Army and SCAP should agree, the ECA representative will have a
delicate task in controlling and reorganizing the eristing SCAP economic staffs.
C
In view of SCAP's loyalty to his staff, it is hard to imagine replacement of
Gen. Marquat and the top members of his staff while SCAp is still there. The
introduction of another agency into Washington policy-making will also have its
complications, though the Army would step out of the recovery field as ECA
stepped in. This raises the cuestion whether the State Department should take
over the remaining non-military occupation responsibilities in Washington from
Army if there is an ECA take-over. The possibility of a treaty next spring or
874.50
sunmer makes it doubtful, however, whether thi would be worthwhile.
To avoid a division of responsibility and to induce General MacArthur to
acgept the turn-over to ECA, it must remain clear that he is the supreme U.S.
authority in Japan. The appointment of General MacArthur as ECA representative
with an ECA Deputy, as suggested by E, may be the best way of effecting this.
July 1, 1950, the cormencement of FY 1951, appears a reasonable date for
the turn-over if the necessary agreement can be achieved.
Conclusions
Turnover of economic responsibilities for Japan from Army to ECA would have
definite

=== Page 255 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
definite advantages, but not so great as to justify a struggle by the State
Department against Army and SCAP objection.
Rather than send letters to ECA and Army, as recommended by E, it is
suggested that Mr. Butterworth should discuss the matter with Mr. Voorhees
and obtain his reaction. If generally favorable, ECA and then SCAP can then be
brought into the discussions.
Attention should in any case be given to the use of sufficiently flerible
language in the appropriation act to allow transfer of funds to ECA during
FY1951.

SUBJECT: Tokyo's 679 of September 29, 1949.
A sumrary of Japan's economic problems was prepared by
Lr. E. R. Lingeman, Minister (Commercial) United Kingdom Liaison
Miscion in  for Mir. Malcolm MacDonald, Commissioner General
for the United Kingdom in Southeast Asia. Lingeman observes that
Japan is "hag-ridden" by tihe problen o feeding an increased popu-
lation. The imprcved economic conditions ince 1946 and the steady
rise in industrial prodt ction are due to
"the four-fold increase of U.S. procurement as
tetween 1947 and 1048 . . . . and deriving, of
cource, fren increaced Cor gressienal appropri-
ations."
In comnentin on the Dee e Mission, Lingeman stated that the
results of the Mission heve ": een renarkably rapid with a reduction
in note isrue, the flattening of the price spiral, ard the inaugura-
tior of e policy of "inar cial retrenc ment and strict control of
credit, interued to provoke tie rationalization of industry" as well
as the establishmert of an of icinl exchane rate of yen 360 to 1.
ir. Linrenan comnents tiat the successes now visible may te attributed
to "the shrewaness ano determinatior of Mr. Dod e."
There is a camain on foot, tacked b. the Jupanese Economic
Stabilization board to reverse the stabilizatior profram. Mr. Lingeman,
however, does not trink tiat Jeyan will be able to ie in to live off
its own resources as earl. as lof3 without considerable assistance from
the United States.

Subject:
Views of Social Democratic Party and Econo-
mic Stabilization Board with Regard to
E
Economic Stabilization.
8cm
894.
The Honorable
894.50/41-249
Secretary of State,
Washington.
Sir:
I have the honor to report on the views of the
Economic Stabilization Board and the Social Demo-
cratic Party with regard to the economic stabiliza-
tion program and the so-called "Dodge Line" for
fiscal retrenchment. In so far as possible the
material below is based on studies and commentaries
elucidating those views; the Department will note.
however, that, in view of the fact that the Social
Democratic Party's program is vague in the extreme,
much reliance has been placed on interpretation and,
to some extent, speculation.
In brief, the announced position of economists
in the Economic Stabilization Board (ESB) and the
Social Democratic Party is that the Yoshida Govern-
ment has gone beyond the Dodge program in proposing
debt retirement, and that, within the framework of a
balanced budget, steps should be taken for increased
public works, unemployment relief, and the provision
of eguipment funds to basic industries. Officially,
therefore, the two groups advocate changes in the imple-
mentation of the program rather than a revision of the
progrem itself. For reasons which will be indicated
later, however, it is not unlikely thet they would
welcome a revision of the "Dodge Line" itselr, but
avoid a direct atatement of that aim for obvious pol1-
tical reasons.
Views
JAN 25 1950

Tokyo's Despatch No. 76i,

-2-
Views of the Economic Stabilization Board
For a competent summary of the ESB's position
as contrasted with that of the Winistry of Finance,
which presently has the upper hand, there is enclosed
11
a memorandum prepared by Mr. Leonard FELSENTHAL, eco-
nomist in the Division of Research for the Far East,
Office of Intelligence Research, who recently completed
a period of temporary duty in Seoul and Tokyo.. As
appraised by Mr. Felsenthal, stabilizetion "Plan A,"
which has the support of the Finance Ministry and of
the Prime Linister, "is based primarily on the elimin-
ation of inflationary factors and the discouraging of
expansion of investment by the government". "Plan B",
which is supported by the ESB, "is based on a gradual
expansion of investment as a prerequisite of final
stabilization".
The differences between the two prcgrams, it will
be seen, revolve around the following points: both
propose industrial rationelization, but Plan B advo-
cates spreading the costs over & long period; Plan A
would freeze wages, while Plen B, sanctioning limited
price rises if worid prices rise similarly, would pre-
sumably elso εllow limited wage rises to prevent a
fall in reel wcges; Plan A would discourage capital
accumulation by the Government and limit public works
severdy, while Plan B would encourage Government
investment and advocates public works to a minimum
total of 100 billion yen for Japanese fiscal year 1950;
Plan A tightens Government and private credit, while
Plan B edvocates government loans and long-term agencies
for extending credit to medium und small enterprises.
Although it is not explicitly so stated in Mr. Felsen-
thal's summary, Plan B also contemplates wider direct
investment of U. S. Aid Counterpart Funds than hes so
far been the case, and would not oppose in principal
credit expansion through sme agency similar to the
now inactive Reconstruction Finance Bank.
A somewhat more deteiled statement of the Econo-
mic Stabilization Poard's position is provided in a study
2/
dated August 1, 1949, and entitled "Analysis of Eccno-
my in Transition", five copies of which are enclosed.
In brief, the study analyzes the deflationary effect
of the l949 budget, and notes possible countermeasures
which can be taken within the framework of the balanced
budget.
The heart of the deflationary problem is seen in
the balanced budget, which on the one hand produces a
flow of money into the Bank of Japen and e consequent
shrinkage
CONFIDENTIAL

=== Page 264 === (confidence 0.99)
November 4, 1949.
CÓNFIDENTIAL
-3-
shrinkage in industrial funds, and on the other
reduces purchasing power by increasing the tax bur-
den and forces prices up by reducing subsidies. The
trend is aggravated by the reluctance of banks to
extend long-term euuipment loans, or to provide credit
to small and medium enterprises, and by the suspen-
sion of Reconstruction Finance Bank loans, which have
hitherto been the mainstay of such essential indus-
tries as coal mining. U.S. Aid Counterpart Funds,
moreover, assertedly have not been made available to
fill the gap between supply and emand for equipment
funds.
The slump in Japen's expcrts, the study oon-
tinues, is of course largely the resuit of factors
beyond Jepanese control, but may in part be explained
by internal pressures. Thus, export financing
becomes difficult us tne credit situatlon tightens;
production declines as stockpiles accumulate (and the
eccumulstion of stockpiles in turn is in part due to
the lessening of effective demend and the shortage
of equipment funds), and production costs consequently
rise; and, finally, exrort industries, except in the
cases of a few large and favored companies, are
unable to obtain funds with which to carry out ration-
alization programs.
As a result, stockpiles accumulate, payments lag,
real wages fall, medium and small enterprises face
increasing financial difficulties, nd, for want of
long-term credit, dismissal of employees becomes the
only possible way of adjusting the situation. Thus
the economy stagnates, and, the study concludes,
"it is feared that the adjustment of e conomy for the
short-term profitability /i.e., abrupt fiscel retrench-
ment/ may result in the vicious circle cf economic
shrinkege bringing bout further economic shrinking.
From the long-range viewpoint of econcnic self-support.
Japan:s economy sheuld not be of such small scale as
to consune not aore than three million tons of coal
or & hundred and fifty thousand tcns of steel per
month"
After this detailed analysis of deflationary trends
and potentialities, the study concludes vith the rela-
tively mild sugfestions thet Counterpart Funds become
available, and that government investment be increased
"within the framework of the balanced budget". "In
short", it is noted, "an expansion of investment in
the basic industries at present will result in an even-
tual saving of aid to Japan".
No attempt is made here to indicate more than the
barest essentials of the Economic Stabilization Board's
thesis. As a well-organized statement of a dissident,
if not hostile, view of present economic policies, the
study is considered worthy of more than perfunctory
attention.
Other

Other Statements
With the announcement of Mr. Joseph DoDGE's
expected return to Tokyo, the Japanese press and
periodicals devoted an increasing amount of space to
appraigal of the so-called "Dodge Line", and to pre-
dictions and suggestions regarding its future appli-
cation. of particuler interest wes a series of
erticles by leading economists published in the
Nihon Keizai Shimbun under the title "What we Hope
for from Mr Dodge" Among the contributors were a
number of economists whose views in general coincide
with those outlined abcve. who are more or less
closely identified vi th the Social Democratic Party,
and who have carried considerable weight with the
Economic Stabilization Board. Translations of three
of the more significant articles are enclosed; a brief
statement of the views conteined therein follows.
Mr. WaDA Hiroo, former direcvor of the Economic
Stabilization Boerd and at present director of the
31
Policy Investigation Bureau of the Sccial Democratic
Party, expresses doubts as to whether implementation
of the program by the present Government conforms to
Mr. Dodge's intentions, and points to the "super-
balanced" budget as evidence that those intentions
have been perverted. To counteract the decline in
production and the accumulation of stockpiles, he
recommends maximum use of the Counterpart Fund for pub-
lic works, unemployment countermeasures, and assist-
ance to local finance, as well as curtailment of the
present program of redeeming Jovernment and Recon-
struction Finance Bank bonds, and, of course, abandon
ment of the "super-belanced budget" To increase
effective demand, he proposes lightening of the
income tax burden and reising of the wage base for
public servants. Assailing the present.rogram as
"financial and big-business stabiiity". e calls for
a more even distribution of the secrifices.
Mr. INABA Hidezo. fcrmer Econcnic Stabilization
Board economist who resigned upon the unnouncement that
4/
the Economic Rehabilitation Planning Committee's
"Five Year Plan" (this Mission's despatch no. o69 of
September 26, 1949) would not be published, notes the
same general evidences of economic stagnation as did
the ESB in the study summarized above. Additionally,
he points out certain indications that actual unem-
ployment has increased much more sharply tnan would be
indicated by relief statistics. While believing the
economic stabilization program to be necessary and
unavoidable, Mr, Inaba suggf ts the following modifi-
cations in its operation: increased public investment,
and increased direct investment from the Counterpart
Fund, together with loan guarantees as required;
minimum

There are indications. however t.t the Econc-

Party would faver relaxation f the "odge Line"
itself. The enclose! re ort on the eeonomy in trensi-

trend, which statement in effect lays all the blame
for the deflation on the budget itselr, rather than

Department's attention is also called to the concluding

. CONFIDENTIAL
-6-
memorandum: "The questions raised by the 'plenners'
are whether Janan will be able to achieve this stabi-
lization at the level indicated above without the
stimulant of rising prices, and within the mechanism
of private enterprise and free competition".
Althougi completely up-to-date statistics are
not available, there is some evidence that real wages
have declined slightly in recent months. The Social
Democratic Party must in the final unalysis support
wage increases, and whether or not declining wages
sre a necessary result of the Dodge program, it is
very unlikely that wages can rise unless the infla-
tionary spiral is resumed. Therefore, while it is
no doubt true, as Ir. Felsenthal points out in the
enclosed study, that there is general agreement on
the principle of stebilization, thet ugreement pro-
bably does not extend to absolute acceptance of the
"Dodge Line". It is possible that the economists noted
above. as well as the Social Democratic Purty itself.
have concentrated their attack on the Government's
enforcement of the Dodge program in the knowledge that
it would be politically inexpedient to attack the pro-
gram itself and perhaps in the telief thet some
relaxation must inevitably come if present wage
trends continue. In any event, it is difficult to
believe that the Party. in view of the colicies to
which it is committed can be a devotee f the Dodge
plan, and, in view of its position as the leading
non-Conmunist opposition to the present Government.
and as the most important single element resisting
the polarization of Japanese politics, it is believed
that the Party's views on economic stahilization
should be recommended to the Department for close and
continued observation
The Depertment is invited to ive rticular
attention to Lir Tsuru': enclosed articie which
recommends that. within a very clearly defined frame-
work of American aid. the Japtnese Govornment be
given complete resronsibility for econusic recovery.
Should that recommendation be sdopted, it would
obviate the present unfortunete situation whereunder
the Cabinet avoids responsibility for unpopular poli-
cies. however much it may opprove of them. It is not
improbable thet the Government would be unwilling or
unable to face the political opposition that would
result from continued enforcement of the present line
if its sole responsibility were made clear.
Respectfully yours,
W J. Sebald

I. Preface
The enronie infletion after tne  p has been linetly "roosve y te en-
forcement of the balanced budgets and the establishment of  tnyl exchan-
ge rate, and thus Japan's economy has made a long stcide towars mormaliza-
tion. Our next turget is to expand quickly production end xpusts on the
basis of the stabilised economy in accordance witi. cconomic prineip les.
In the meantime a chain of deflationary factors such as cputraction of
currency, stagnation of  roduction, drop in prices, shortag of money in en-
ferprises, lack of purchasing power, increase of unemployment, stc. has
started to manifest themselves recently. It is only natural that sueh de-
flationary factors have come to appear in the wake of the long chronic in-
flation, and it would be almost impossible to normalize Japan's economy vith-
out a transition period of this.kind.
Inasmuch as, however, the frailty of Japan's economy-surplus labor,
shortage of incigenous resources, poor accumulation of capital, high depend-

=== Page 285 === (confidence 0.97)
ency on forign trade, etc. -has been accentuated in postwar, it would be as
much or even more dangerous than infl tion if the present deflationary trend
following the stabilization develops beyond the limits of normal adjustments.
In other werds, if Jepan's econamy which has lost much of its flexibility
after the wor secvuse w the extremely low inoove level were exp sed to a
severe do lati  it is vory likely that the basis of Japan's ec n my itself
ou whict cntmrprise rati nalization is vs be attained cullapses by the
impact f increasing s cial conflicts.
In 1948 Sir Staff rd Cripps, Chancellor of the Exchecker, stated in his
address  the budgets t the efrect that it would be necessary to prepare
thenselves s that they might be able to catch the moment when a deflation-
ary trend ruplases tne fading inflationary pressure and to take immediate
steps for the adjustments of acononic end fiscal policies.
The present Japan's econamy.may be likened to a sericus patient to whom a
big operation has been just administered. We have to endeaver to avoid the
advont ef -crisis by analyzing the changing economic trend promptly and care-
fully and taking appropriate countar-measures commensurate with the changing
circumstances.

2. Analysis of Deflationary Factors.
a. Factors in the Field of Public Finance, Money and Ranking.
The key-note of the recent deflationary trend in the inancial
field is reflected in the contraction of currency, which is mainly attri-
butable to the budgets for the 1949 fiscal year. Although it is very
plain that the tight-money situation has been aggravated by such factors
as strict loan policy of general financial institutions, suspension and
promotion of collection of the Reconstruction Finance Pank loans, the
fundamental cause of the tight-money situation may bo found in the
formulation of the new budgets.
(1) Nature of the Budgets for the F.Y. 1949 and their Enforce-
ment.
The budgets for the F.Y. 1949 is noteworthy in their attain-
ment of a true balance but they contain a possibility of causing a deflation-
ary trend. The deflationsry factors of the said tudgets consist of currency -
contraction to be found in the composition of the budgets themselves
(cuper balanced tudgets), reduction of the purchasing power through tax
collection and substantiel decrease in government investment.

4. Social impact cf derlation
The deflationary tendeney has just begun to show its sign on the
national livelihood. Real wages began to fsll in end after April and
living standard in citles fairly declined in June. Tnrough the mountlng
difficulties of medium and small enterprises, particulary in machinery
inlustry, rationalization of industries, administrasive retrenchment, ecc.,
the number of the unemployed is tncreasins and there te a denper et cocial
unrest being brewed up. In case the above tendency is left unattended, the
deflationary tendeney in industries stated in 3 above will inevitsbly make
iteelf felt in national livelihood through the inererse in unymployment,
decrease in real wages, bankruptcy of medium end smail ent rpris s, uper
econg
zation of farm village,  etc. There is a pessibilicy that ratienalisctien
of enterpriaes is effected at the sscrifice of national livelihood. In
view of the fact that the present living standard is extrenely low, the
most serfous problem involved in deflation must be said to lie in the
stove possibility. Let us nor review the social impaet of deflation as
anneered in statistical figures.

The Acting Political Adviser has the honor to enclose a copy
of a statement entitled "American Aid to Japan," issued November 9,
1949, by the Public Information Office, General Headquarters, Far
East Command. Toiye. It will te noted that this statement was pre-
pared by the Programs and Statistics Division, Economic and Scien-
tific Section General lieadquarters, Supreme Commander for the
Allied Powers.
According to this statemer  a basic shift in the scope of
American id -c apan has ured since its inception following
the termination f ostilities. The initial intention was to pre-
vent disease and uires uring recent months the concept of
American aii as a re ief  pelhabilitation measure has been super-
seded by an int ention to ss Unitei States appropriated funds prim-
arily cwards he s a f  talanr ed ec onomy and to cover the de-
Picit in Japan s lere f payments.

AMERICAN ATD TO JAPAN
(Prepared by the Eronomic and Scientific Section's Programs
and Statisties Division, GHQ, SCAP)
A basic shift in the siope of American Aid to Japan has occured
since its in eption fcllowing the termination of hostilities.
The iritia  intention primarily, was to prevent disease and un-
rest wiich might endanger the occupation forces or prejudice the suc-
cessful accomplishmert :f their mission. Clearly, however, though
Japan's irst  ritirai meed was to secure a minimum level of subsis-
t ence, ss cf her en:my required immediate measures for
econoni: vehani itati and beginning with the budget for fiscal
year 94? unts f r that specifi: purp se were appropriated. This
made  sit e th- pr  u: euen: of producers gcods as well as the sub-
sistence t-ms to hyr expenditures of American Aid funds for Japan
hat leen arg- y vte However, it was inereasingly realized that
no m     y been dislocated, but her tra
di   a    o  eenony of the Far East as a

s apa tesh a ew equilibrium both internally
and ex erre here c, luring recent months the concept of American
Aid as  re e  elatlor measure has been superseded by an
intention tc use United States appropriated funds primarily towards
the goal of a balarced economy and to cover the deficit in Japan's
balance of payments, This probably will be reflected in fewer limita-
tions over he types and the quantity of essential commodities to be
procured in the future from American Aid funds for Japan.
The underpinning of the Japanese economy provided by American
Aid imports has lately been reflected in increased supplies distri-
buted through rationing channels. Textile fabrics available for
household use have increased from .38 pounds per person during calen-
dar year 1948 to .89 pounds during 1949. Also imported staple foods
have made possible an in rease in the proportion of rice released
through rationing.
American Aid imperts int: Japan increased during the first half
of 1949 over the previous six-month period, although the proportion
of American Aid to ctal imports declined. During the latter half
of calendar year 1949, both an absolute and proportional increase in
Americar Aii is projected.

I.
Bumnary
The long persisted inflation in our ccuntry whiuh had been losing
its force since last year entered at last into its final stage with
the putting into effect of the economic stabilization program es
embodied in the formulation of the consolidated balanced budgets and
the establishment of the exchange rate. The issuance of the Bank
Notes kept on decreasing from 355.3 billion yen at the end of last
year to 29s.; billion yen at the end of July, this year and has re-
mained around that level. It has come to resume its normal sea-
sonal fluctuations es in pre-yar pericd. As regards prices, offcial
prices are still on a gradual rise due to the reduction of subsidies,
the establishment of exchange rate, etc., but blackmarket prices
began failing after ths beginning of this year. Effective prices
which are the combination of officiel end blacknerket prices have
been kept on more or less the same level since the start of this
year. The average wage of industrial workers kept on rising by
about l0% per month up till the end of lest year but it hes beensalmost
st bilized on the lavel of 8400-3500 yen per month in this year.
The abeve trends of currency, price and wage eloquently speak for the
recent stabilization of economy.
In the first half (April through September) of this fiscal year
the Government hed en exccss revenue of 29.1 billion yen over pey-
ment as a result of the execution of the balanced budgets.
On the other hand financing was temporarily tight in April and May
when our economy underwent major adjustments but the lendings by
finencial institutions have since recome active owing to the positive
operation by the Bank of Japan and to the smooth increase in bank

=== Page 453 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2-
deposits and postal savings. The additional credit extension by the
Bank of Japan in the first half of this. year amounted to 32.4 billion
yen (10.7 billiun-yen if excess withdrawal of the Government's design
ated deposit is deducted) which was sufficient to counteract the
budgetary factors ceusing currency contraction and to maintain dis-
inflationary line. The above trend shows that the stabilization
program which originally aimed at restoring function of financing
from Government to monetary institutions is getting realized.
On the other hand, the disappearence of additional purchasing
power born of inflstion in proportion to the stabilization of economy
has served to lessen the disequilibrium between demand and supply of
commodities, to bring blackmarket prices down closer to official
prices,
enabling thereby a substantial repeal of allocations and
other ecomomic contr: ls and steadily expanding the sphere of free
competition in the economy.
Furthermore, the realization of stabilization and the expansion
of free competition have enabled in wide area the promotion of enter-
prise rationalization, the increase in labour productivity, the im-
provement in quality of products, the reduction in cost, etc. For
example, the national average coal production per face miner per day
increased from 1.44 tons in July last year.to 1.67 tons in July this
year. Taking into sccount the reduced working hours (roughly from
10 hours to 8 hours per day) brcught about by the Labour Standards
Law, the productivity of miner is steadily approaching the prewar
level. The improvement in the quality of coal is also noticeable.
The average calorie of ooal purchased by the National Railways rose
from 5540 calories in August last year-to 6000 calories in August
this year, thus enabling a l0% saving in coal consumption needed for
the transport of a fixed volume.

=== Page 454 === (confidence 0.97)
- 3 -
The execution of the stabilization program has thys played a
major role in norr alizing the economy end paved. the way for entoring
the international eccnomy through the improvement ia efficiency and
the reduction of cost. However, from a long-term riewpoint of eco-
nomic rehabilitation, it should be looked with corsern that tle de-
clinging effective demand and mounting stockpiles.aused by the
sluggish export due to the transition in world marets and by the.
slash in domestic investment due to the requiremen of the stabili-.
zation program are cousing a stalemate or slight d@ecline in industrial
production.
kocent figures of foreign trade show that theactual export
since the beginning of this yeer hes kept the levu: of 45 million
dollars per month which is almost double. the amout in last yee'r..
contracts
But the amount of export in the second half of th year declined as
compared with the first half and remsins at a leel slightly above
30 million doll rs per month. As a result of th devaluation of
the pound the export contracts concluded from l& to 2oth of October
was no more than 18million dollars. With the Iguidation of su-
picion as to the yen devslustion, the alolitin of floor prices and
the implementaticn of heasures for trade promddon, such as the simpli-
ficatien of-the export and import procedures, te.future
expansion
of export can be expectcd. In vier of the fa that the fundemental.
idea of the stebilization proeram is to promote export and to acce-
lerote economic self-support by curbing domest purchasing power,
the success or failure of export expension wil have a far.reaching
effect upon the stabilizetion progrem.

=== Page 455 === (confidence 0.98)
The actual import since the beginning of this year has meintain
the level of about 80 million dollers per month but the recent import
contract has declined to 60 million dollars per month. The percentage
of export contrect against import contract in January through September
was 77% as compered with the 38% in the corresponding period of last
year, showing thst a good progress toward self-support has been made
in the last twelve months. The stalemate in import, however, is a
reflection of the domestic productive inactivity. In crder to achieve.
a healthy eccnomic self-support much higher export and import-levels
should be attained.
Tne domestic investrent level hes shown a sizable fall owing.to
the big slash-in c pital expenditure in this year's budget (forrail-
ways, cormnunications, public works, etc.), suspension of the financing
by the Reconstruction Finance Bank, delay in the lending of the U.S.
Aid Counterpart Fund, etc.
The construction accounts of the Nationel Railways and Tele-
communications  in this year show a declinc of 48% and 27% respective-
ly as compared with last year, if állowance' is madd of price increase.
The supply of equipment fund for private industries (excluding reserves
in companies) is estimoted at 26.9 billion yen in the first hslf of the
ear (April thr ugh September) and around 80 billion yen for the whole
yesr including the probable jarger release of the counterpart fund in
the latter half of the year, indicating a 40% decline as compered with
the preceding year.
Tne above reduction of investment had an advefse direct effect on
the capital goods industries end lowered their operational rates. and
wersened their accounts es is shoyn by the fact that the personnel

weather hate necessitated the increased allocation of expenses for

.The number of dweliing

The reduction of public works expenditure and the sluggish non-Govern-
mental construction activities have hard hit the construction industry
and are causing speedy licuidation of the medium and small sized
construction contractors, in particular.
The reduction of investment, stagnation of consumption level and
 the slugish export have led to the shortage of effective demand and
to. the increase of stocks and uncollected sales proceeds. and are
giving rise to difficulties of enterprise management. The industrial
production which had risen by 41% from April last year through March
this year became stalemate from April and continued to be so until
September. In September this year the industrial activities index
(average of 1932-1936: 100, including utilities) and the industrial
production index (not including utilities) ccmriled by EsS SCAP
were 93.2 and 78.4 respectively.
With regard to the impact of the stabilization program on enter-
prises, awmoniun sulphate, oil and fats, paper industry, etc. which
hsve secure domestic market are n relatively favourable conditions
but the capital goods and export industries are suffering from the
lack of demand and sluggish sales. As a result, such industries

=== Page 457 === (confidence 0.98)
-.6 -
as are hardest hit, e.g., reilway rolling stock, -wire tele-com-
munication, motor car, etc. are slashing 20-40% of their personnel.
In spite, however, of the above. adverse condition, enterprises, parti
cularly big ones, have come out relatively unscathed and have managéd
to keep their heads above the water. This may be attributed to the
following'factors:
Firstly, in the ccurse of lest year there was a sizable increase
in production which, by raising the operational rate, reduced the
cost of production. In consequence thereof official prices turned
out to be pretty generous for efficient
producers who have succeed-
ed in accumuiating some reserve.
Secondly, bank loans were made more actively than expected.
For big enterprises,in particular, banks lent fairly liberally despite
the increasing stocks and credit. sales balance...
Thirdly, Banks and enterprises alike have expectation that
 things wili turn for the. better from next year with the expansion of
export, release of the counterpart fund, incr-ase of Government in-
vestment, etc.
In case, therefore, demand fails to increase next year there is
a'possible danger thet they collapse suddenly under the accumulated
burden. The rolling stock industry is e good example.. It is striying
to get through the adverse situation created by the drastic decrease
in demand by personnel cut, reduced wages, sub-contract from other
firms, borrowings frcm banks, etc. in the hepe that demand will in-
crease by export expansión and increased appropriation for Government
investment in next year. Should its expectation be unfulfilled it
be confronted with a sericus situation.

The above situation is reflected in the lendings by banks.
The apparently smooth lendings are being made chiefly for furnish-
