financings for increesing production and modernizing production
methods are limited.
The enterprise rationalization is elso being effected-chiefly
by personnel cut, closure of unprofitable factories, reuction of
sub-contract prices to .small enterprises, etc.
There are few
cases of wholesome rationalization through modernization of eauip-
ment and induction of new technique.
As regards the medium and small sized enterprises, it cannot
be denied that the lack of effective demand and the revival of
free competition have made their position less favorable in view
of the quality of their products and their financial capacity.
Inasmuch as their employees occupy 70% of the total industriel
employees they cannot be left unattended also fron the social
standpoint.

staple food prices following the r vision of the parity index, 32% rise on
the avorage in the prices of 67 textile goods following the establishment
of a single exchang rate, 12% rise on the average in the prices of 25
items of fuels and 37% rise on the average in the prices of 10 iteme of
metals, ccused tais l0% rise.

3. Normalization of economy.
Stabilization anl normalization of economy has been reflected on
which are main ecsn mic indices and the price structure is being rapidly
reorganized. This movement may be sgen from the fact that the price struc-
ture is approaching that in the world ma ket and that the gap between the
blackimrket and official prices is dwindling or in other words dual price
etructure is diminishing.
The establishment of a single exchange rate has rapidly brought the
prices of oxport g ods in line with the internetional prices. Tne maximum
price ratio was brought down to 450 yen on 15 February ad to 425 yen on

This situation is being reflected upon price control which is rapialy
narrowed in coverage. 64,506 items. (subclassification) were under price
control in April this year. But they rapidly decreased month after month
and items placed off control up to the end of September totalled 26, 023
or 40% of the total. Decontrolling is expected to continue also in the
fuburo.
Distribution control is also being narrowed in coverage. There were
284 designated production materials in April this year. Since then 148
items have been decentrolled, used copper and 19 other items being the
the first, and coal and coke being the last to be decontrolled. Control
is still maintained on 136 items. In the near.future, however, 'more items
may be decontrolled loaving basic and impurted materials. Rationing
control on cunsumer gosds govered 70 items at first. But 39 itcms includ-.
Ing fruit and vegutables ha been decontrolled, leaving 31 items including
staple food and textile gonds. Along with relaxation of cortril, method
of control has been modified. Consumers selectfon; returned coupon system.
advance order sytem otc. leaving greater room for free csmpetition have
been adopted. Moreover, to cope wi th the lack of purchasing power,

Of. 15 kodans, ive Kodans including the Soild Fuel Distribution Kadan

and consolidate kodans int n loaving only five Kodans.
Control is being gralually relaxed also in other fields sach as

decontrol but what control to mintain. The basis of free economy
besed on the principle of producers competition and consumers choice
is being gradually enlarged.

The rationalization of enterprises has made headway, as. aeen b
as the stabilization program progressed. However, it has so far been
achieved mainly through such measures as elimination of excessive emplo
concentration of productin, cutting down of purchase prices of produot
of med:um and small sub-contractors, etc., but scarecely through such
fundamental means of rationalization as modernization of equipmont,
introduction of new techniquo, etc. More often, enterprises are endeavor
ing to improve their imaediate financial status, at the sacrifice of
their future competitive power, by sparing repair and maintenance of.
existing equipment and closing or reducing research and experiment institu
tes. On the other hand, the current rationalization has entailed vari-
ous social problems by reducing employment and increasing unemployment
r art-time employment.

Foreign trade is an indispensable mainstay of the economic rehmbs

restraining domestic consumption and urging export, thereby induoing.rehn
bilitation and self-support of the national economy. Much depends, ther
fore, on the export, and it has been the fcous of attention of the pation
The state of foreign trade is reviewed in the following pages.

In addition to the aforementioned obstacles to export on the
domestic side, there are other obstacles of foreign origin.which
should not be overlooked. The foreign market competition has been
intensified on account of the slump in world market lead by the
American sconomy, shift from "sellers' market" to "buyers' market
induced by the economic recovery of European countries, import restri-
ctions of various countries necessiteted by the shortage of dollar,
political and economic unrest in Asia, and so forth. The impart. of su
deterioration of foreign market is all the more serious for Japan
on acccunt of hor randicaps in competition, such as the absence of
indepenuence in forcign trade, lack of means to adapt herself to forei
market, complication of trade procedures. The devaluation of pound on
18 September and'suosequent similar steps taken in other areas added
further difficulties to the export trade. The amount of export con-
trects concluded be twoen the storling area for the period from 19
Septembor to 16 Octo or was only 1.2 million dollars, or 8% of the
monthly average of 15.4 million dollars for the period from May to
August. The export to the sterling aree which occupied 45% of the tote1
export during the latter four month period dropped to 4% in the 19
September to 16 October period. Such remarkable shrinkage in the export
to the sterling areu is, of cource, an irmediate reaction to the pound
devaluation, and tie oxport contracts with the pound area during the
period irom 1st to 25th of Octoner hus restored to-2.5 million dollare
Tho futurt changes, thercfore, call for careful attontion.

b. Public Works and Agricultural Investment.
It is natural that Japan is liable to natural calamities on.
account of her geographical nosition and the damages left unrehabili-
tated hecause of the reduction of disaster prevention expenses since
the China Incident have been gradually accumulated with the result.
that rivers are liable to flood even by slight rainfall every.year
causing to river works, crops:and farms, houses and other construc-
tions, household articles, etc. the danages anounting to several times
as much as the prevention expenses required. The total damages caused
by natural calamities amounted to approximately. 2oo billion yen in
the last fiscal year and it would not be the exaggeration to say
that the rehabilitation of calamity-stricken areas and the disaster
prevention are of the basie requirements for the reconstruction of.
Japan's economy.
However, in the budget for the 1949 fiscal year the public
works expenses were reduced to 50 billion yen or 67% in substance
of those in the 1948 fiscal year along the policy of reducing public
investment.
Thus even the expenses for the rehabilitation of calamity-
stricken areas on which the greatest inportance was placed in asses-
swent were reduced to 16.9 billion yen or 62% in substance.of those
in the last fiscal year. These expenses are for the rehabilita-
. tion of the damages caused in the preceding years and no expenses. for
the rehabilitation of the damsges in the present fiscal year are
included in the present budget.

=== Page 505 === (confidence 0.99)
-51-
In the meantime as much as 68.5 billion yen of State grant
is estimated to be required for the rehabilitation of the damages caus
ed by the typhoons (Della, Fay, Hester, Irma, Judith, Kitty, ete.
in the present fiscal year.. Thus dilapidation of the national land
is still being aggravated, affecting productive capacity, especial-
ly that of agriculture.
Although the public works expenses were considerably redoed
as stated above, special stress was placec on such disaster preven-
tion items as river works, anti-torrentials, afforestation, etc. in
the reduced public works expenses in view of the magnitude of repeated
calamities with the result that such items as agriculture (provided
for the prisent fiscal year with only 52% of the expense for the
last year), housing (58%), city rehabilitation (46%), and educational
facilities (13%) were most severely reduced. In the case of educa-
tional facilities, such severe reduction is almost tan tamount to
the total suspension of construction of new facilities for 6-3-3
educational system.

Next, how the reduction of public investment as stated above
is affecting &griculture will be studied.
Farming in Japan is extre-
mely small-scale end it is being intensified by ever increasing popu-
lation.

=== Page 507 === (confidence 0.98)
-53 -
Thus, as the result of the reduction of agricultural invest:
ment in the budget for the present fiscal year, the farmers, unable
to bear the burden with their own small economic resources, was
forced to look foward to local public finance and private loens.
However, it is almost impossible for hen to obtain loans from general
financial institutions as their sums are sulstantial and their terms
are required to be low interest and long term (7 to 20 yeafs) in
order to make repayment with crop while farm land is not allowed to
be used as security at present. Furthermore, the Deposit Bureau
funds are not made available for agricultural investment while not
much can bc expected from the Aid Fund (2.5 billion yen is listed for
agricultural investment in the operational program of the Aid Fund
for the present fiscal year).
In this way, the raduction of public works expenses and the
failure to cover the resulting gap with long-term agricultural finane-
ing by the Central Bant for Agriculture end Forestry, etc. are causing
considerable stagnation in various agricultural constructions in the
present fiscal year. For example, while construction is either delayed
or suspended in 18 areas out of 26 aress under national land reclama-
tion program, construction is entiely stopped in <15 areas out of
925 areas under land riclamation program in Hokkaido and 1,224 areas
out of 1,874 areas under prefectural reclamation program. Further-
more, construction is suspended in 28 areas out of 90 areas under

=== Page 508 === (confidence 0.98)
-54 -
sea and swamp reclamation program. Even in the field of irrigation,
drainage and land improvement which are very effective in the- expan-
sion of agricultural productive power the effects were considerable
and while the aree to be placed under irrigation and drainage works in 
the present fiscal year is expected to be reduced to 120,000 "cho"
(300,000 acres) or one third of that in the last fiscal year the
area to be placed under land improvement program in the present fiseal
year is estimated to be reduced to 3,ooo "cho" or one eighth of that
in the last fiscel year. Thus, the expansion or maintonance of agri-
cultural productive power indispensable to the reconstruction of
Japan's economy is coming to impasse.
As seen in the case of agriculture stated above, local finance
is also affected by thc reduction of public works expenses and local
governments are endesvouring to tide over the situation with the in-
creased collection of local taxes and contributions. However, it
would be almost inpossible to expect much fron the local governments
highly dependent on the national. Government especially as the local
bond issue is limited at 23 billion yen (19.7 billion yen exelusive
of the issue for the last fiscal year) in the present fiscal year.
Therefore, the investment for construction, both netional and local,
for the prusunt fiscal year will be forced to remain at the level of
about 70% of that in the last fiscal year. In this way it would be
impossible not only to stop further dilapidation of the national land

=== Page 509 === (confidence 0.98)
-55.
and make a step toward the reconstruction but also to improve much the
present circumstances in which housing is short as many ss 3,600,000
units because of wer damages and population increase and 30% of:
schoolboys cannot be sufficiently accommodated.
It may be added that resignation or recall cf many chiefe of
cities, towns and villages in connection with the suspension of
various construction programmos and financiel troubles was one of the
repercussions caused by the reduction of public works expenses.
3. Shortage of effective demand
The curtailment in purchasing power of the public finance and
disbursement of eouipment fund consequential of the promotion of
the stabilization program on the one hand and the slump in.export
trade on the other have br ught about the shortage of effective
derand and invited a state of relative excess of production through-
out the whole economy. The present situation may be called "plenty
in poverty", not "poverty in plenty", when the fact is taken into
consideration that the level of mining and manufacturing production
stands only at 70 to 80% of the pre-war level. Following are some
of the indic:tions of the shortage of effective demands.
a: Incrense in stockpiles

Iv Effects of the Implementation of the Stabilization Program.
A. Effect on businesses.
1.
Generel view by industry.
The recent trend of business following the implementation of the
stabilization pro ram will be analysed below seperately with regard
to basic industries, capital goods industries, and export and con-
sumer goods industries.
a. Basic industries.
Coal.
As a result of fall in effective demand, the recent
actual delivery is below two million tons a zonth. A stockpiling
tendency which began on low grade coal in September last year has
now
spread to high grade coal. Up to last September, produc-
tion was not below the plan tecause producers tried to produce as
much as possible in the expectation that the Kodan would be aboli-
shed. After coal was 'placed under free sale, all mines paid more
attention to rationalization, restricted production of low grade
coal and intensified the strict selection of coal with the result
that coal production in October fell to S9% in the first ten days
and to 78% in the second ten days as against the target of 42 mil-
lion tons. After decontrol
, the prices of high grade coel are a
bit. risins but those of low grade coal fell sharply. On the
average, 10-20g fall is noticed. The quality of coal has pemarkably

=== Page 526 === (confidence 0.99)
-71---
improved, raising the consumption efficiency in coal consumping
industries such as railways, electric power supply industry etc.
On the other hand, efficiency of each mine under free oorpetitfon has
/higger
become considerably clear. It is estimated that quite a number of
suall and medium-sized mines willhavo to restrict production or to
stop operation. Coal dealers will meet considerable difficulties
in selling coal until the disposal of/million tons of stockpile
/4.8
held by the Kodan is completed. As a result of discontinuation of
Reconstruction Finance Bank loans, and of the abolition of the
Kodan, the coal industry has come to expereince the same financial
difficulty as the other industries. 3.6 billion yen of joint loan
was extended for the payment of debts. Put only the big five compa-
nies were benefitted by this loan. The out-standing debts of coal
mines incurred in, connection with business operètion amountto 14
billion yen, thet related to equipment, 3.2 billion yen in equip-
ment, and that related to coal miners housing one billion yen.
These debts have far-reaching effect on related industries such as
iron and steel, machinery, dynamite and timber industries.
Electricity.
The current electricity rate is far below the general
price level as stated before. Electric power industry can hardly
pay for coal, repeir of equipment and other current expenses.
The amount of coal for thermal power generation counted into the
cost on which the current electricity charge is based is 3.7 mil-
lion tons cr 950,000 tons 1ess than 4.65 million tonsplanned to be
used for thermal power this year.
The industry is
fiding it difficult to find money to buy coel with the dry winter

however, only to a limited extent that income and expenditure can
be balanced through these efforts. The equipment funds required
for the current year 1s about 26 billion yen. (17.4 billion yen
last year) 13.1 billion yen of this is expected to be provided by

oven after the first half of the current fiscal year is already

2. Recent labcur and employrent situation
The process of rstionalization bogan with personnel reduction.
Accordint to the employment survey of the Ministry of Labour, em-

show a sigri of decline in January.
Employmant in all industries
declined by 0.25% in Feburary (as compared with the preceding month)
and 0.29% in March, and after a teniporary increase of 0.59% in April
on account of the recruit of school graduates, continued to decline by
O.ć3% in May, 0.9% in June and 3.1 of in July. The branches which
showed remarkable declire in July were coal mining (7.O4% -- percen-
tage decline in July as compared with January; same hereunder),
machinery (22.06%), sill reeling and weaving and other textile spinning
and weaving (9.07%), lumbering and woodwork (10.7%), transportation
and communications (11.78%),etc.
The "State of Enterprize Readjustment" of the Ministry of Labour
reveals that the tempo of personnel reduction quickened sherply in
and after the month of Nay, especially in Júly.
This is chiefly
due to the reduction of investment, in consequence of which machinery
industry which was directly affected and all other industries were
more or less degrees forced to discharge employees. The first wave
of personnel reduction in consequence of the economic stabilization
program, has subsided after the peak in July. At the same time,
the Government pereonnel.cut numbering es much as 200,000 was com-
pleted during the months from July to September. Under these cir-
cuastances, it may be said thst the first wave of personnel cut as
a whole has ccme to an end.
The future development of personnel
cut depends upon the tempo of economic rehabilitation.

3. Trend of farmers' ecenomy
The prosperity of farmers' economy brought about by the rise in
prices of foods after the termination of the war has now ceased with
a fow exceptionel cases and farmers' economy is gradually turning
to depression. Such trend -lreedy set in the course of last year,
and it is not appropriate to attribute it to the economic stabili-
zation program. Nevertheless, there is a possibility in future
that the normalization of economy and retionalization of enterprise
in the cour se of the eccnomic stabilization programwill be carried
out by shifting difficulties to farmers' economy:
There have
already been a number of indicatons to this effect.
One of the major factors is the increase in the population
pressure to agriculture. The figures of the employed populatian on
farm are as follcws. According to the Labour Force Survey of
August 1949, the employed population on farm is 19,150,000, or 12%
increase over the corrcsponding period of last year and 52% ofthe
total working population. (The latter percentage was 48% in August
last year and 40% in 1940.) The main causes for this increase are
the flow of orkers discharged by the rationalization of enterprises
or administrative retrenchment intc/rural districts and the shift of
non-agricultural labour force to agricultural lsbour force (for
example, in case workers oommuting fron rural districts to towns
for non-agricultural enployment.lose their jobs, it will result in

=== Page 555 === (confidence 0.98)
the increase in the egricultural labour force), in addition
the natural increase and the inflow of the repatriated.
agriculture, however? is extremely intensive and the agricult
labour force hes been excessive. A part of the increased 
force, therefore, becomes unemployed and other pert partially
ployed.
The number of the portially employed on farm has rema
increased this ycer as compared with thet in the corresponding
last year as mentioned previcusly in connection with employment,
at the same time, the rate of their increase is far greater than
that of full-time workers, showing increase in latent unemployment
on far.
The shrinkage of blackmarlet transactions following the.normal
zation of. economy affects the farmers' econmy. Although blackmarket
parchase of production materials and daily necessities in rural dis-
tricts has been declining year by year, the blackmarket sale of foods
decreased to a large extent in consequence of the Increase in the
food ration and of stagnation in pruchasing power in cities, farmers
economy whichhave depended considerably on the food biackmarketing
having thus been affected sdversely. For exemple, the percentage
of expenditure for blackmarket purchase against the total expenditure
for staple foods in C.F.S. (Tokyo-to) Mas fellen remarkably from

V. Future problems
We have revlewed the general situation of our economy since the 
mentation of the stabilizatlon program. Following are some of the.majo
problems which we will face on our road to the attainment of sound and mn
supporting economy.
1. Export and production
The Johnston Report released by the United States Department of the
Army in April last year states: "It has been estimated that Japan will
require about $1,575,000,000 of exports each year (at current prices) to
pay for essential imports of food and raw materials (and necessary invi-
sible imports) to maintain a tolerable food ration and standard of living
at home." The present level of exports has to be raised to four
fold in order to attaln such goal of exports.
Essential conditions for the enhancement of exports are the prevention
of recurrence of inflation and promotion of rationalization of enterprises
on the internal side and the solution of market problems and improvement
of trade conditions on the external side. It is needless to say that
adaitional efforts should be made to improve the internal condltions, but
no less ussential is it'to better the external ones. First, it is necessarg
that tho Far astern countries which are our main market for exports should
be able to pay ror the imports from Japan. For this purpose, our imports
from these countries should be increased on the one hand and necessary
fund made/available for them for the development of resources. Second,
/solnehow
our people should, at the earlie t opporcunity, be allowed to partiolpate
in tne International trado on equal footing with other nations. Uur
people are suftoring from various restirictions in their efforts for
export promotlon and in trade conditions. The handicaps will become all
the more serious as sellers' competition gets intensified in the worid
market.

=== Page 559 === (confidence 0.98)
-104-
While the mining and manufacturing production has recently been
gnant, the current level of production is far too low to sabisfy the
of export promotion, enhancement of investment and future improvement 
living standard. The production level needs be raised by 60 to 70% to
the attainment of a self-supporting sconomy. In the ciroumatanoes where
there is a large potential demand for the modernization of equipment,
prevention of the deterioration of the national land, promotion of exporte
etc. on the one hand and an excessive supply of labor on the other, we
cannot allow coal consumption to remain at the level qf 3,0o0,00o tons.and
steel consumption at tne level of 150,000 tons per month. Moreover, as
the industrial pr ducts--machinery, metals, chemical products, eto.-in.part-
icular-will occupy increasingly larger portion of our exports in future,
silk exports and invisible trade incomes having decreased to a large extent,
the reduction of production costs through raising the operatlon rate of
enterprises is all the more necessary to enhance our exports. We must,
therefore, raise the level of production by expanding exports and domestio
investment demand.

a. Reduction and abolition of subsidies
While the reduotion and abolition of subsidies is strongly regu
by the public finance and also is an important step in the normalization
of economy, its impact on the over-all actual economy should fully be tak
into account. The problems with regard to iron,steel and sode are as foll
food and fertilizer having been reviewed previously.
As a result of desubsidizetion in September, the official prioe
was raised for iron by 70%, 35% for steel and 40% for caustio soda. The
step has so far stimulated the selfsupport of the industries without 
posing excessive burden on enterprises. Nevertheless, the offiolal prices
have, as a result of this raise, reached, or surpessed in some instangee,
the level of the blackmerket prices, indicating future problems for des-
ubsldization. Tha: is to say, as the recent shrinkage in effective demand
makes it difficult to raise officials prices of products to full extent,
the margin has to be absorbed by enterprises themselves through their
rationalization. But the question is that owing to the stagnant produo-
tion there is scarcely any room to reduce their costs of production by
raising the rate of operation. Moreover, they are nt a digadvantage in
the acquisition of imported m turials in that they, not being able to
import from the Far Eastern areas on account of political unrest there,
are obliged to import from distant areas, paying unusually high freight.
As long cs it is necessary to keep up these industries to maintain domestio
employment and to aconomizc sur imports on the one hand and to utilize
the resources of the Far Eastern countries which are our major market
and to enhance their capacity to import our products on the other, the
subsidies in these industries should be reduces or abolished only in
consideration of the rise in their operation rate and the improvement of
their conditions of raw materials Import. To put it in anotner way, the

b. Revaluation of assets
The Shoup Recommendation releascd in September will induce funda-
mental ohanges in jur taxation system. Its effects upon the management of
enterprises are extensive and complicated, some of the major points of
which are the revaluation of fixed assets and texation on the revaluation
profits, the abolition of the excess profits tax, the expansion of the
real estate tax, the revision of the cnterprise tax (adoption of the value
added tax), the abolition of the transaction tax, etc. In particular, the
revaluation of assets is an important step which stops the disinvestment
of capital long continued under inflation and clarifies the profit conditions
of enterprises, thoreby promoting the normalization of the economy. There
arise similar problems in the revaluation of assets as in the case of
desubsidization. That is. to say, enterprisers have to bear the increase
in the cost of dopreciation and the burden of the rovaluation tax unless
they manage to shift them to consumers by raising the prices of their
producte. If they cannot raise the prices of their products on account
of the shrinkage f demand and the low level of international prices, as
has been pointed out in connection with desubsidization, they have to
absorb the increasud costs of production through their rationnlization.
Suppose production remins stagnant at the current level in next yoar,
it is feared that those enterprises, wnich have large asscts but operate
at low rate and have poor profit rate, will suffer from financial burdens
for a year or two and will nave thoir reconstruction delayed. Under such
circumstances, it is likely that onterprises revalue their assets as low

as possible in order to escape thèf? fmmediate difficulties
if the real estate tax be imposed in direct relation to the revalna
amount. Although Dr. Shoup pointed out the danger of excessive remaib
the danger in the current economic circumstances is under-revaluatlon
rather than over-revaluation. It is deemed necessary that the immediat
burden on enterprises accompanying revaluation be aot too heavy,becaua
under-revaluation will continue consumption of capital and disturb effort
for rationalization.

3. Rationalization of enterprises.
As explained in the foregoing chapters, the rationalization
of enterprises will have to be pushed foreward more and more inten
sely. But the rationalization through the elimination of excessive
employment, concentration of production, price reduction of sub-con-
tracts, etc. is approaching its limit, as mentioned above. More-
over, the strengthening of buyers in the market has weakened the
producers' position to such an extent that producers often accept
orders at prices below production costs simply to maintain opera.
tion. Such situation has come to prevail especially in machinery,
rolling stock and slipbuilding and other capital goods manufactur-
ing branches, because of their limited domestic market and of
extremely meagre supply of equipment funds. It may be tha't the
elimination of unsound enterprises through such process may promote
normalization of economy, but the problem lies in the fact that
the present level of effective demand is still so low as compared
with the necessary level in self-supporting economy that the capa-
city of production surplus at present is not necessarily surplus
j
in future, and thet the survival of enterprise depends more often
on the volume of capital power or on good connection with financial
institutions than on the superiority of management. Furthermore,
in order to rationalize individual enterprises, it is necessary that
the entire economy be simultaneously rationalized. For 'example,
the reduction of interests rate of financial institutions is one
of the important factors, and the reconstruction of commercial
structure is another.

=== Page 565 === (confidence 0.99)
-110 -
The scale of commercial functions heretofore undertaken by
the Government or Kodans is diminishing as economic decontrol pro-
ceeds, but private commercial structure to replace the Government
rechanism has not yet fully been established. The interval may
well be bridged by the producers' direct sale system, such as seen
in the sale of coal, but this is merely a temporary measure. It
is necessary that some specialized commercial structure be estebli-
shed. Particularly, inasmuch as Japan has a large number of minor
producers, which fact weakens sellers vis-a-vis buyers, commercial
cooperatives, commercial financing, etc. are very important problems.
The same situation exists as to export industries, in which the
absence of appropriate commercial mechenism put medium and small
enterprises in a very disadvantageous position. Here is another
room for further rationalization of economic structure.
As stated above, the rationalization of enterprises does
not and with the rationalization of individual enterprises alone,
but can be achieved ultimately only through the restoration of mutual
proportions of various branches of industry, normal economic circula-
tion of the entire economy and reasonable intercourse with the inter-
national economy. To force normalizetion of merely a segment of
economy does not only not achieve the normelization of that segment
but will, if pushed to strongly, disturh the reasonable development
of the entire economy. This is why the reduction and abolition of
subsidies and repeal.of cconomic controls should be timed properly
with various factors in the actual cconomy.

Conclusion
The enforcement of the economic stabilization program has throu
sound finance and financing, stcpped the currency increase and pric
rise and succeeded in almost curbing the inflation. But the unex-
pected slump of export and the slash in investnent have caused the
shrinkage of effective denand and resulted in the inoreasing stcektle
and the stagnant or declining production level. Meanwhils, keep-
ing pace with the stabilization of economy,rationalization of enter-
pr:ses has been effected and improvements have been made in pro-
duction efficiency, costs as well as in quality of products. But
the stagenant pr duction caused by insufficient purchasing power has
set a limit to tne rationalization. Those who lost their jobs in
the couree of rctionelization have submerged in cities ard villages
as the half-erployed or unemployed, thus leeving soriovs sociel
proplems, for f ture solution.
Ineswuch as our eccncry needs high level of investment in
order (1) to rehabilitate and replote the war-damages (damages on
domestic assets being 4.2 trillion yen) and capital doprociation
in and after tue wan (2) to vodernize praduction cquipment so that
the goal of export ($1,575,000, 000 as cstimated by tho Johnston
Report) necessary to acliove a self-supporting economy be atteined,
and (3) to sustain the increasing population (about ten million
increase since the end of the war), the decrease in investment
may slow down ths pace of ruhabilitation and reduce tue scele of
future self-supporting econony. Nevertheless, the oojective of 
rehabilita tion and self-support cunnot oe. achievod without etabli-
shing a tough oconomy which can stand the international competi-
tion thrcugh painstaking efforts for stabilization and re tionaliza-
tion.
We shall therefore have to convert the present stagnation into
a strong spring-board for the future development guidod by our
